The **Nagorno-Karabakh conflict**[^cite_note-63] is an [ethnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_conflict) and [territorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_dispute) conflict between [Armenia](10918072.md) and [Azerbaijan](746.md) over the region of [Nagorno-Karabakh](65220.md), inhabited mostly by ethnic Armenians until their [expulsion](74922032.md) in 2023, and [seven surrounding districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian-occupied_territories_surrounding_Nagorno-Karabakh), inhabited mostly by [Azerbaijanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijanis) until their expulsion during the 1990s. The Nagorno-Karabakh region was entirely claimed by and partially controlled by the [breakaway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_states_with_limited_recognition) [Republic of Artsakh](1000530.md), but was recognized internationally as part of Azerbaijan. Azerbaijan gradually re-established control over Nagorno-Karabakh region and the seven surrounding districts.

Throughout the Soviet period, Armenians in the [Nagorno-Karabakh Autonomous Oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagorno-Karabakh_Autonomous_Oblast) were heavily discriminated against. The [Soviet Azerbaijani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Azerbaijan) authorities suppressed Armenian culture and identity in Nagorno-Karabakh, pressured Armenians to leave the region, and encouraged Azerbaijanis to settle within it, although Armenians remained the majority population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStarovoytova199724-64] Motivated by fears of cultural and physical erasure under [government policies from Azerbaijan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Armenian_sentiment_in_Azerbaijan#Suppression_of_Armenian_culture_in_Nagorno-Karabakh),[^cite_note-\:142-65][^cite_note-66][^cite_note-Eduard_Abrahamyan22-67] the [Karabakh Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karabakh_movement) advocated for the reunification ("[miatsum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miatsum)") of the enclave with [Soviet Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic). A referendum in 1988 was held to transfer the region to [Soviet Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Armenia), citing [self-determination](29269.md) laws in the [Soviet constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Constitution_of_the_Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-69] The Karabakh Movement was met with a [series of pogroms against Armenians across Azerbaijan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Armenian_sentiment_in_Azerbaijan#During_the_Karabakh_Movement_\(1988-1992\)), before violence committed against both Armenians and Azerbaijanis occurred.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStarovoytova1997vi-70]

The conflict escalated into a full-scale [war in the early 1990s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Nagorno-Karabakh_War) following the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union). The war was won by [Artsakh](1000530.md) and Armenia, and led to occupation of regions around Soviet-era Nagorno-Karabakh. Azerbaijan and Turkey responded with a transportation and economic [blockade of Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish%E2%80%93Azeri_blockade_of_Armenia_\(1989%E2%80%93ongoing\)) which persists to this day, while Artsakh was also blockaded until 2023. There were expulsions of ethnic Armenians from Azerbaijan and ethnic Azerbaijanis from Armenia and the Armenian-controlled areas.[^cite_note-71] The [ceasefire ending the war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishkek_Protocol), signed in 1994 in [Bishkek](4554.md), was followed by two decades of relative stability, which significantly deteriorated in the 2010s. A [four-day escalation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Nagorno-Karabakh_conflict) in April 2016 resulted in hundreds of casualties but only minor changes to the [front line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_Contact_\(Nagorno-Karabakh\)).

In late 2020, the large-scale [Second Nagorno-Karabakh War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Nagorno-Karabakh_War) resulted in thousands of casualties and a significant Azerbaijani victory. The hostilities subsided with a tripartite [ceasefire agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Nagorno-Karabakh_ceasefire_agreement), under which Azerbaijan captured one-third of Nagorno-Karabakh and regained control over the occupied territories surrounding the region.[^cite_note-Geybullayeva-2023-72][^cite_note-73] [Ceasefire violations in Nagorno-Karabakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peacekeeping_operations_in_Nagorno-Karabakh#Ceasefire_violations) and on the [Armenian–Azerbaijani border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenia%E2%80%93Azerbaijan_border_crisis_\(2021%E2%80%93present\)) continued following the 2020 war. Between 2022 and 2023, Azerbaijan escalated its blockade of Nagorno-Karabakh using a military checkpoint, sabotaging civilian infrastructure, and targeting agricultural workers. The ten-month-long [military siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_Nagorno-Karabakh) isolated the region from the outside world.[^cite_note-\:13-74] In September 2023, Azerbaijan launched a large-scale [military offensive](74857564.md),[^cite_note-offensive-reuters-75][^cite_note-offensive-politico2-76][^cite_note-offensive-france24-77] [expelled the entire population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_of_Nagorno-Karabakh_Armenians), dissolved the government of [Artsakh](1000530.md),[^cite_note-inter_2024-23][^cite_note-\:4-78] and incorporated the territory into Azerbaijan. In August 2025, Azerbaijan and Armenia signed a US-brokered [peace agreement](79518584.md) in Washington, DC, aiming to end the conflict, reopen transport routes, and normalize relations.[^cite_note-79] Barriers to a comprehensive and lasting settlement include Azerbaijan’s denial of the [right of return](74922032.md#Azerbaijan's_denial_of_the_Right_of_Return) for displaced Armenians, disputes over Armenians detained,[^cite_note-\:5-80] and Azerbaijan's demands that Armenia concede an [extraterritorial transit corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zangezur_corridor)[^cite_note-\:6-81] and amend its constitution and national symbols.[^cite_note-\:7-82][^cite_note-\:8-83][^cite_note-\:9-84]

## Background

Main articles: [Armenian–Azerbaijani war (1918–1920)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian%E2%80%93Azerbaijani_war_\(1918%E2%80%931920\)) and [Karabakh movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karabakh_movement)

Following the breakup of the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire), the Armenians of Nagorno-Karabakh formed an unrecognised polity known as the [Karabakh Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karabakh_Council) in 1918. Due to Azerbaijani–British pressure, the Karabakh Council in August 1919 was forced to provisionally recognise the authority of [Azerbaijan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan_Democratic_Republic), pending the [Paris Peace Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Conference_\(1919%E2%80%931920\))'s adjudication of the international borders of the republics within the [South Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Caucasus).[^cite_note-NKR-1918-85] As the peace conference was inconclusive regarding Nagorno-Karabakh, the Azerbaijani governor-general [Khosrov bey Sultanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khosrov_bey_Sultanov), issued an ultimatum to the Armenians of Karabakh in early 1920, stipulating their acceptance of permanent inclusion into Azerbaijan. Leaders associated with the [Republic of Mountainous Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Mountainous_Armenia) and the [Dashnak Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dashnak_Party) attempted to organize a rebellion against Azerbaijani rule, which failed and led to the [massacre and displacement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shusha_massacre) of [Shusha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shusha)'s Armenian population.[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-88] By 1921, Soviet authorities were in control of Nagorno-Karabakh who decided on the formation of the [Nagorno-Karabakh Autonomous Oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagorno-Karabakh_Autonomous_Oblast) (NKAO) within [Soviet Azerbaijan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Azerbaijan).[^cite_note-89]

In 1964, the Armenians of Karabakh sent a letter to leader of the [USSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USSR), [Nikita Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev), complaining about Azerbaijan's economic management of the region. They also requested that the NKAO and adjacent regions where the Armenian population is approximately 90% be reincorporated into the [Armenian SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_SSR) or integrated into the [RSFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RSFSR).[^cite_note-90] The Nagorno-Karabakh Armenian intellectuals who wrote to the Kremlin were imprisoned, put under surveillance, or forced to leave Nagorno-Karabakh. All lost their jobs and were removed from the Communist Party.[^cite_note-circle1-91]

> I tried to change the demographics in Karabakh. We encouraged Azeris living in neighbouring regions to move to Karabakh instead of to Baku. I made efforts to ensure that there would be more Azerbaijanis and less Armenians living there.

— [Heydar Aliyev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heydar_Aliyev) in a 2002 interview.

[^cite_note-circle1-91]

Throughout the Soviet period, [Azerbaijani authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) implemented policies aimed at diluting the Armenian majority in Nagorno-Karabakh through various means, including border manipulations,[^cite_note-\:0-92][^cite_note-Levon_Chorbajian,_Patrick_Donabédian,_Claude_Mutafian-93][^cite_note-Christopher_J._Walker-94] encouraging the exodus of Armenians, and settling Azerbaijanis in the region.[^cite_note-\:1-95][^cite_note-Patrick_Wilson_Gore-96] Census data from the Soviet period reflects these demographic shifts.[^cite_note-\:10-97] [Shusha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shusha)—once a major center of Armenian cultural life—became overwhelmingly Azerbaijani.[^cite_note-\:10-97] In the 1920s, Armenians constituted 94.4% of Nagorno-Karabagh’s population, with only 5.6% being Azerbaijani; by 1979, Armenians' share had fallen to 76%, while the share of Azerbaijanis had increased to 24%.[^cite_note-98]

This [discrimination against the Armenian population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Armenian_sentiment_in_Azerbaijan) was part of a part of a broader strategy to suppress Armenian culture and identity in Nagorno-Karabakh. Former Azerbaijani [President Heydar Aliyev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heydar_Aliyev) later admitted to supporting some of these policies.[^cite_note-\:2-99][^cite_note-\:3-100] Teaching Armenian history in Nagorno-Karabakh was banned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStarovoytova199724-64] Nagorno-Karabakh Armenians were persecuted for choosing to attend college in Armenia instead of Azerbaijan or for supporting the [FC Ararat Yerevan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Ararat_Yerevan) football club instead of [Neftchi Baku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neftchi_Baku). The [1973 Soviet Top League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Soviet_Top_League) final in which Ararat Yerevan defeated [FC Dynamo Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Dynamo_Kyiv) was not aired in Azerbaijan SSR, and Nagorno-Karabakh Armenians that listened to the match by radio were arrested and questioned by the [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB).[^cite_note-circle1-91] Despite these pressures, Armenians remained the majority in Nagorno-Karabakh when the USSR collapsed.[^cite_note-101]

Amid [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev)'s reforms the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) in 1988–89, a 1988 Nagorno-Karabakh referendum was held to transfer the region to [Soviet Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Armenia), citing self-determination laws in the [Soviet constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_Soviet_Union). In response, a series of pogroms were committed against Armenians throughout Azerbaijan, leading to the start of the Nagorno-Karabakh War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStarovoytova1997vi-70] Professor Matt Qvortrup considered it hypocritical that Western Europe countries had eagerly recognised the succession of several states from [Yugoslavia](34244.md), ignoring the laws of territorial integrity, but simultaneously did not show the same interest for the Nagorno-Karabakh referendum, noting "the practice of independence referendums seemingly owes more to national interest than to adherence to principles of [jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisprudence)".[^cite_note-102]

According to Stuart Kaufman, a professor of political science and international relations,[^cite_note-103] and de Waal, the first instance of violence in the conflict occurred in October 1987 when an Azerbaijani official "punished" the Armenian-populated village of [Chardakhly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%87ardaql%C4%B1,_Shamkir) with a raid for protesting against the appointment of a new collective-farm director.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Waal200318-104] During the raid, the village's women, children, and elderly were beaten up.[^cite_note-kaufman-105] In his 2003 book *Black Garden*, de Waal speculated that "\[p]ossibly in reaction to such incidents", Azerbaijanis in Armenia (specifically in the [districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_the_Armenian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) of [Ghapan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghapan) and [Meghri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meghri)) were in November 1987 driven out of their homes, arriving at [Baku](4566.md) in two freight cars.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Waal200318–19-106] A number of Armenian scholars and investigative journalists, however, have scrutinized these alleged incidents and argued that, outside the claims made by Azerbaijani officials, there is no evidence, archival or otherwise, to corroborate such instances of mass violence taking place, at least prior to February 1988.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECheterian2008104–108-107][^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109]

## Timeline

### First Nagorno-Karabakh War (1988–1994)

Main article: [First Nagorno-Karabakh War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Nagorno-Karabakh_War)



Graves of Azerbaijani soldiers

The First Nagorno-Karabakh War, also known as the *Artsakh Liberation War* in Armenia and Nagorno-Karabakh, was an armed conflict that took place in the late 1980s to May 1994, in the [enclave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enclave) of Nagorno-Karabakh in southwestern Azerbaijan, between the majority ethnic Armenians of Nagorno-Karabakh backed by the Republic of Armenia, and the Republic of Azerbaijan. As the war progressed, Armenia and Azerbaijan, then both former [Soviet Republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republics_of_the_Soviet_Union), entangled themselves in a protracted, undeclared [war in the mountainous heights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_warfare) of Karabakh as Azerbaijan attempted to curb the Armenian [Karabakh movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karabakh_movement).

On 20 February 1988, the [enclave's parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_\(Artsakh\)) voted 110-17 in favor of uniting with Armenia.[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Waal201311–12-111] The demand to unify with Armenia, which began anew in 1988, began in a relatively peaceful manner. As the Soviet Union's dissolution neared, the tensions gradually grew into an increasingly violent conflict between ethnic Armenians and ethnic Azerbaijanis. Both sides made claims of [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing) and [pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogrom) conducted by the other.[^cite_note-CASE-STUDY-IN-ETHNIC-STRIFE-112][^cite_note-113]



Photos of fallen Armenian soldiers in [Stepanakert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stepanakert), Nagorno Karabakh

The circumstances of the dissolution of the Soviet Union facilitated an Armenian [secessionist](144732.md) movement in Soviet Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-Croissant-114] As Azerbaijan declared its independence from the Soviet Union and [removed the powers held by the enclave's government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_on_Abolishment_of_Nagorno-Karabakh_Autonomous_Oblast), the Armenian majority of Nagorno-Karabakh voted to secede from Azerbaijan. [The referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Nagorno-Karabakh_independence_referendum) held in 1991 was boycotted by the Azerbaijani population and had an electorate turnout of 82.1%, of which 99.9% voted in favor of independence.[^cite_note-Geybullayeva-2023-72][^cite_note-NKR-1918-85] The referendum resulted in the [unrecognized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_unrecognized_states) [Republic of Nagorno-Karabakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Nagorno-Karabakh).[^cite_note-115]

Full-scale fighting erupted in the late winter of 1992. International mediation by several groups, including the [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md) (OSCE), failed to bring resolution. In the spring of 1993, Armenian forces captured territory outside the enclave itself, threatening to catalyze the involvement of other countries in the region.[^cite_note-116] By the end of the war in 1994, the Armenians were in full control of most of the enclave and also held and currently control approximately 9% of Azerbaijan's territory outside the enclave.[^cite_note-117] An estimated 353,000 Armenians from Azerbaijan and 500,000 Azerbaijanis from Armenia and Nagorno-Karabakh were displaced as a result of the conflict.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Waal2013327-118] A Russian-brokered [ceasefire](592591.md) was signed in May 1994, leading to diplomatic mediation.[^cite_note-Freizer-2015-119]

### Border clashes (2008–2020)



The situation in the area after the 1994 ceasefire

The [2008 Mardakert clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Mardakert_clashes) began on 4 March after the [2008 Armenian election protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Armenian_election_protests), resulting in several score wounded and killed, with both sides declaring victory.[^cite_note-bbcnews2-120][^cite_note-bbcnews-121] It was the heaviest fighting between [ethnic Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagorno-Karabakh_Defense_Army) and [Azerbaijani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_of_Azerbaijan) forces since the 1994 ceasefire after the First Nagorno-Karabakh War.[^cite_note-bbcnews2-120][^cite_note-bbcnews-121] Following the incident, on 14 March the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) by a recorded vote of 39 in favour to 7 against adopted [Resolution 62/243](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_62/243), demanding the immediate withdrawal of all Armenian forces from the occupied territories of Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-122] The [2010 Nagorno-Karabakh clash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Nagorno-Karabakh_clashes) was a scattered exchange of gunfire that took place on 18 February on the line of contact dividing Azerbaijani and the Karabakh Armenian military forces. As a result, three Azerbaijani soldiers were killed and one wounded.[^cite_note-123] The [2010 Mardakert clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Mardakert_clashes) were the deadliest for Armenian forces since the 2008 violence.[^cite_note-RFE-124] Between 2008 and 2010, 74 soldiers were killed on both sides.[^cite_note-crisis3000-55]

In late April 2011, border clashes left dead three [Artsakh Defence Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artsakh_Defence_Army) soldiers,[^cite_note-125] while on 5 October, two Azerbaijani and one Armenian soldier were killed.[^cite_note-deadly-126] In all during the year, 10 Armenian soldiers were killed.[^cite_note-tolldown-127] The following year, [continued border clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Armenian%E2%80%93Azerbaijani_border_clashes) between the armed forces of Armenia and Azerbaijan took place from late April through early June. The clashes resulted in the deaths of five Azerbaijani and four Armenian soldiers. In all during 2012, approximately 20 Azerbaijani and 14 Armenian soldiers were killed.[^cite_note-disputed-128][^cite_note-restraints-129] Throughout 2013, 12 Azerbaijani and 7 Armenian soldiers were killed in border clashes.[^cite_note-disputed-128]



The [breakaway republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_states_with_limited_recognition) of Abkhazia, South Ossetia and Nagorno-Karabakh within the Caucasus region, between 2008 and 2020

In 2014, [several border clashes erupted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Armenian%E2%80%93Azerbaijani_clashes).[^cite_note-rferl1-130][^cite_note-131] By August, 27 Azerbaijani soldiers had died since the start of the year.[^cite_note-euras-132] On 12 November 2014, the [Azerbaijani armed forces shot down](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Armenian_Mil_Mi-24_shootdown) a Nagorno-Karabakh Defense Army [Mil Mi-24](384591.md) helicopter over Karabakh's Agdam district.[^cite_note-133] With the crash, 2014 became the deadliest year for Armenian forces since the 1994 ceasefire agreement, with 27 soldiers killed.[^cite_note-reporter.am-134] Six Armenian civilians also died in 2014, while by the end of the year the number of Azerbaijanis killed rose to 39 (37 soldiers and 2 civilians).[^cite_note-restraints-129] In 2015, 42 Armenian soldiers and 5 civilians were killed as border clashes continued.[^cite_note-attrition-135] In addition, at least 64 Azerbaijani soldiers also died, according to Armenian sources.[^cite_note-10months-136][^cite_note-contact-137]

Over the years, propelled by oil and gas revenues, Azerbaijan embarked in a military build-up. In 2015 alone, Baku spent US\$3 billion on its military, more than Armenia's entire national budget.[^cite_note-openDem-138]

In early 2016, the most serious clashes until the 2020 war occurred (the [2016 Nagorno-Karabakh conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Nagorno-Karabakh_conflict)). Between 1 and 5 April 2016, heavy fighting along the Nagorno-Karabakh frontline left 88 Armenian and 31–92 Azerbaijani soldiers dead. One Armenian and three Azerbaijani soldiers were also missing. In addition, 10 civilians (six Azerbaijani and four Armenian) were also killed.[^cite_note-at92-139][^cite_note-meydan-140] During the clashes, an Azerbaijani military helicopter and 13 unmanned drones were shot down and an Azerbaijani tank was destroyed, while Nagorno-Karabakh lost 14 tanks.[^cite_note-13th-141][^cite_note-surgeon-142][^cite_note-143]

[Continued clashes occurred in 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Armenian%E2%80%93Azerbaijani_clashes).[^cite_note-144] Three civilian volunteers were killed in a demining operation in Nagorno-Karabakh on 29 March 2018.[^cite_note-145] 2020 saw a number of clashes, particularly heavy in July ([July 2020 Armenian–Azerbaijani clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_2020_Armenian%E2%80%93Azerbaijani_clashes)).[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147]

### Second Nagorno-Karabakh War (2020)

Main article: [Second Nagorno-Karabakh War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Nagorno-Karabakh_War)



A truck with the slogan "Karabakh is Azerbaijan" at the [Baku Victory Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baku_Victory_Parade_of_2020) on 10 December 2020. The parade was held in honor of the Azeri victory in the 2020 conflict.

Large-scale fighting began on the morning of 27 September, with an Azerbaijani offensive[^cite_note-russiamatters01-148][^cite_note-eurasianet_290920-149] along the [line of contact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_Contact_\(Nagorno-Karabakh\)) established in the aftermath of the first war. Clashes were particularly intense in the less mountainous districts of southern Nagorno-Karabakh.[^cite_note-150] On the same day, Azerbaijan's Parliament declared a [martial law](50477.md) and established [curfews](297681.md) in several cities and regions following the clashes.[^cite_note-151] Turkey provided military support to Azerbaijan, although the extent of this support is disputed.[^cite_note-russiamatters01-148][^cite_note-152]

The war was marked by the deployment of [drones](58900.md), sensors, long-range heavy artillery[^cite_note-Gatopoulos-2020-153] and [missile](19594.md) strikes, as well as by state propaganda and the use of official social media accounts in online [information warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_warfare).[^cite_note-Mirovalev-2020-154] Azerbaijan's widespread use of drones was seen as crucial in determining the conflict's outcome.[^cite_note-155] Numerous countries and the [United Nations](31769.md) strongly condemned the fighting and called on both sides to de-escalate tensions and resume meaningful negotiations.[^cite_note-156] Three ceasefires brokered by Russia, France, and the United States failed to stop the conflict.[^cite_note-157]

Following [the capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Shusha_\(2020\)) of Shusha, the second-largest city in Nagorno-Karabakh, a [ceasefire agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Nagorno-Karabakh_ceasefire_agreement) was signed, ending all hostilities in the area from 10 November 2020.[^cite_note-RIANovosti-158][^cite_note-RIA_Novosti-159][^cite_note-BBC_News-2020-160] Following the end of the war, an unconfirmed number of [Armenian prisoners of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_POWs_during_the_Second_Nagorno-Karabakh_War) were captive in Azerbaijan, with reports of mistreatment and charges filed against them,[^cite_note-CEPS-161][^cite_note-ArmenianPOWsAbused-162][^cite_note-ArmenianPOWs-163][^cite_note-164] leading to [a case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Armenia_v._Republic_of_Azerbaijan_\(ICJ_case\)) at the [International Court of Justice](14918.md).[^cite_note-165]



Map of the [2020 Nagorno-Karabakh ceasefire agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Nagorno-Karabakh_ceasefire_agreement)

Casualties were high.[^cite_note-aljazeera-2020-166] According to official figures released by the belligerents, Armenia and Artsakh lost 3,825 troops,[^cite_note-ArmenianKIA-167][^cite_note-McKernan-2020-168] with 187 servicemen [missing in action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missing_in_action),[^cite_note-ArmenianMIA-169] while Azerbaijan claimed 2,906 of their troops were killed, with 6 missing in action.[^cite_note-azMoDlist-170] The [Syrian Observatory for Human Rights](34029669.md) reported the deaths of 541 Syrian fighters or mercenaries fighting for Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-SOHR541-171] However, it was noted that the sides downplayed the number of their own casualties and exaggerated the numbers of enemy casualties and injuries.[^cite_note-jamnews-172]

The total number of reported civilian fatalities on both sides was at least 185; the whereabouts of 21 Armenian civilians remain unknown. Civilian areas, including major cities, were hit, particularly [Stepanakert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_bombardment_of_Stepanakert), [Martuni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_bombardment_of_Martuni), [Martakert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martakert), [Shushi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Ghazanchetsots_Cathedral_shelling) in the Republic of Artsakh and [Ganja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganja,_Azerbaijan), [Barda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barda,_Azerbaijan) and [Tartar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartar_\(city\)) in Azerbaijan, with many buildings and homes destroyed.[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175]

### Border tensions (2021–present)

#### Border crisis (2021–present)

Main article: [Armenia–Azerbaijan border crisis (2021–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenia%E2%80%93Azerbaijan_border_crisis_\(2021%E2%80%93present\))

An ongoing border crisis started on 12 May 2021, when Azerbaijani soldiers crossed several kilometers into Armenia in the provinces of [Syunik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syunik_Province) and [Gegharkunik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gegharkunik_Province), occupying between 50 and 215 square kilometers.[^cite_note-Toal-2022b-176][^cite_note-Khudoyan-2022-177][^cite_note-Stepanian-2023a-178][^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181][^cite_note-Martirosyan-2023a-182][^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] Azerbaijan has not withdrawn its troops from internationally recognised Armenian territory despite calls to do so by [European Parliament](9581.md), [United States](3434750.md) and [France](5843419.md) – two of the three co-chairs of the [OSCE Minsk Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OSCE_Minsk_Group).[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-MacronTweet-22]

Following the end of the Second Nagorno-Karabakh War, Azerbaijan has made numerous incursions into Armenian territory[^cite_note-186] and has regularly violated the ceasefire agreement, provoking cross-border fights with Armenia.[^cite_note-\:15-187][^cite_note-europarl-2021-188][^cite_note-KarabakhSpace.eu-2023-189][^cite_note-Europarl-2021-190] In order to strengthen the border against Azerbaijan's military incursions, Armenia allocated additional defense areas to border guards of the [Russian Federal Security Service](385990.md).[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192]

The crisis escalated in July 2021, with clashes taking place on the Armenia–[Nakhchivan](385358.md) border, and in November 2021 in the Gegharkunik–[Kalbajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalbajar_District) area.[^cite_note-europarl-2021-188] In August 2021, Azerbaijani forces blockaded southern Armenia (Syunik) by closing the main north-south highway which interrupted all international transit with Iran and forced Armenia to develop alternative roads.[^cite_note-Ghazaryan-2021-193][^cite_note-Kucera-2021b-194][^cite_note-Kucera-2021c-195][^cite_note-196]

Although the mission has been condemned by Azerbaijan and Russia, the EU has sent a [CSDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSDP) [civilian monitoring mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Mission_in_Armenia) to Armenia to contribute to stability along the border and deter offensives by Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198][^cite_note-Bedevian-2023-199][^cite_note-Khudoyan-2022-177] The president of Azerbaijan, [Ilham Aliyev](22765442.md), has made numerous threats to Armenia, making statements such as "Armenia must accept our conditions" if Armenians wish to "live comfortably on an area of 29,000 square kilometers," and "they must not forget that Armenian villages are visible from here."[^cite_note-caucasuswatch.de-200][^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204]

The largest escalation occurred in September 2022, when Azerbaijan initiated its [largest attack on the Republic of Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_2022_Armenia%E2%80%93Azerbaijan_clashes) in the [history of the conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_conflicts_between_Armenia_and_Azerbaijan) between the two countries.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-Reichardt-2022-206][^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208] Since Azerbaijan's offensives, Armenia's borders with Azerbaijan have become militarized which has disrupted the livelihoods of residents in border communities: residents have been targeted and could no longer access farmlands, schools, water resources, relatives, or religious sites.[^cite_note-caucasuswatch.de-200][^cite_note-Von_Joeden-Forgey-2023-209][^cite_note-Toal-2022b-176][^cite_note-\:15-187] Fearing for their safety, many Armenian villagers have moved away permanently.[^cite_note-ge1-210][^cite_note-Kucera-2021a-211][^cite_note-212]

Armenia has unsuccessfully requested that the [Collective Security Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_Security_Treaty_Organization) (CSTO) and Russia independently intervene due to Azerbaijan's military incursions in May 2021 and September 2022. The CSTO and Russia declined to provide assistance both times.[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214]

#### Blockade of Nagorno-Karabakh (2022–2023)

Main article: [Blockade of Nagorno-Karabakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_Nagorno-Karabakh)

On 12 December 2022, under the guise of "environmental protests", Azerbaijan launched an illegal [blockade](293054.md) of Nagorno-Karabakh.[^cite_note-BBC2-215][^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217][^cite_note-Gavin-2022b-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] The [Azerbaijani government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Azerbaijan) sent citizens claiming to be "[eco-activists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmentalism)" to block the [Lachin corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lachin_corridor), the only road connecting Artsakh to Armenia and to the outside world.[^cite_note-\:13-74][^cite_note-politico.eu-221][^cite_note-BBC2-215][^cite_note-yahoo-2022-222][^cite_note-Azerbaijani-223] Civil servants, disguised military personnel, members of pro-government NGOs, and youth organisations were among the so-called "eco-activists."[^cite_note-224] Only Azerbaijani journalists from state or pro-government media were permitted past checkpoints into the region.[^cite_note-rsf.org-2023-225][^cite_note-Kitachayev-2023-226][^cite_note-MEYDAN.TV-2022-227][^cite_note-Council_of_Europe-2023b-228]

From 26 March to 30 September 2023, the Azerbaijani government consolidated its blockade: [seized strategic ground around the Lachin corridor both within Artsakh and Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenia%E2%80%93Azerbaijan_border_crisis_\(2021%E2%80%93present\)#Armenian_territory_occupied_by_Azerbaijan), installed a military outpost that blocked a bypass dirt road that provided relief, blocked the old section of the Lachin corridor, and installed a checkpoint at the new section.[^cite_note-caucasuswatch.de-200][^cite_note-229][^cite_note-Avetisyan-2023b-230][^cite_note-Stepanian-2023b-231][^cite_note-Khulian-232][^cite_note-OC_Media-2023b-233][^cite_note-234] Azerbaijan has ignored calls from the Russian peacekeepers to observe the 2020 ceasefire conditions and return to their initial territorial positions.[^cite_note-caucasuswatch.de-200][^cite_note-Gray-2023a-235][^cite_note-Avetisyan-2023b-230][^cite_note-Stepanian-2023b-231][^cite_note-ahmedbeyli-2023-236] Azerbaijan has also ignored calls from the International Court of Justice, the [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights), and other human rights organizations to restore freedom of movement across the Lachin corridor.[^cite_note-ReferenceD-237][^cite_note-hudoc-2022-238]

The blockade had severe consequences for the population: importation of food, fuel, and medicine was blocked, and the 120,000 residents of the region were trapped, which created a humanitarian crisis.[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240][^cite_note-\:15-187][^cite_note-Gavin-2022a-241][^cite_note-Kitachayev-2022-242][^cite_note-crisisgroup-2023-243] Shortages of food, medicine, and electricity were widespread[^cite_note-Gray-2023b-244][^cite_note-Avetisyan-2023c-245][^cite_note-Avetisyan-2023a-246] with emergency reserves were rationed,[^cite_note-Jamnews-2023-247] along massive unemployment[^cite_note-intellinews.com-248][^cite_note-Khulian-2023-249] and school closures.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-CTJ-2022-251]

During the blockade, Azerbaijan has deliberately damaged or cut various critical civilian infrastructure which supplied Artsakh: including gas, electricity, and Internet.[^cite_note-Council_of_Europe-2023a-252][^cite_note-Kouloglou-0075-253][^cite_note-\:13-74][^cite_note-lemkininstitute.com-254][^cite_note-Hill-255][^cite_note-Avetisyan-2023c-245] The region was without gas from 22 March to 30 September 2023[^cite_note-OC_Media-2023c-256][^cite_note-Shahverdyan-2023b-257] and Artsakh authorities have resorted to daily 6-hour blackouts in order to ration the limited local electricity production, as Azerbaijan has prevented repair of a damaged supply line.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-The_Armenian_Mirror-Spectator-2023-259][^cite_note-Hetq.am-2023-260]

Azerbaijani President Aliyev has said that "Armenians living in Karabakh must either accept Azerbaijani citizenship or look for another place to live" and has threatened military action.[^cite_note-Trend.Az-2023-261][^cite_note-ReferenceA-52]

Local Armenian residents feared that the blockade aimed to expel them from their homeland[^cite_note-Reichardt-2023-262][^cite_note-amartikian-2023-263] and various human rights organizations and scholars specializing in [genocide studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide_studies) have warned of [genocide risk factors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Risk_factors_for_genocide).[^cite_note-Genocide_Watch-2022-264][^cite_note-Genocide_Warning:_Nagorno_Karabakh-265][^cite_note-Lemkin-2023b-266][^cite_note-Chidiac-2023a-267][^cite_note-Rhodes-2023a-268][^cite_note-Gregorian-2022a-269][^cite_note-Politico-2023a-270]

### 2023 offensive and expulsion of Armenians

Main articles: [2023 Azerbaijani offensive in Nagorno-Karabakh](74857564.md) and [Expulsion of Nagorno-Karabakh Armenians](74922032.md)

On 19 September 2023, Azerbaijan [launched a military offensive](74857564.md) on Nagorno-Karabakh.[^cite_note-offensive-radiofreeurope2-271][^cite_note-offensive-reuters-75][^cite_note-offensive-politico2-76][^cite_note-offensive-media2-272][^cite_note-offensive-france24-77] One day after the offensive started, on 20 September, an agreement on establishing a complete cessation of hostilities in Nagorno-Karabakh was reached at the mediation of the [Russian peacekeeping command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peacekeeping_operations_in_Nagorno-Karabakh) in Nagorno-Karabakh.[^cite_note-\:11-273][^cite_note-offensive-CNN2-274] Azerbaijan held a meeting with representatives of the Artsakh Armenian community on 21 September in [Yevlakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yevlakh) and a further meeting took place the following month.[^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276] Ceasefire violations by Azerbaijan were nonetheless reported by both Artsakh and local residents in [Stepanakert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stepanakert) on 21 September.[^cite_note-ceasefirevio12-277][^cite_note-ceasefirevio22-278]

Following the military offensive, Azerbaijan [expelled the entire Armenian population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_of_Nagorno-Karabakh_Armenians), dissolved the government of [Artsakh](1000530.md),[^cite_note-inter_2024-23][^cite_note-\:4-78] and incorporated Nagorno-Karabakh into Azerbaijan.

### Azerbaijani resettlement

Main article: [Azerbaijan's construction in areas gained in the Second Nagorno-Karabakh War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan's_construction_in_areas_gained_in_the_Second_Nagorno-Karabakh_War)

Following the Azerbaijani reconquest of the region and the [expulsion of Nagorno-Karabakh Armenians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_of_Nagorno-Karabakh_Armenians), Azerbaijan implemented a program to settle Azerbaijanis in the previously-Armenian-inhabited areas.[^cite_note-279]

## Fatalities

### 1988–1994

An estimated 28,000–38,000 people were killed between 1988 and 1994.[^cite_note-280]

Armenian military fatalities were reported to be between 5,856[^cite_note-AMS-281] and 6,000,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Waal2003285-50] while 1,264 Armenian civilians were also killed.[^cite_note-AMS-281] Another 196 Armenian soldiers[^cite_note-AMS-281] and 400 civilians were missing.[^cite_note-OhanyanVelikhanova-282] According to the Union of Relatives of the Artsakh War Missing in Action Soldiers, as of 2014, 239 Karabakhi soldiers remain officially unaccounted for.[^cite_note-283]

Azerbaijan stated 11,557 of its soldiers were killed,[^cite_note-284] while Western and Russian estimates of dead combatants on the Azerbaijani side were 25,000–30,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Waal2003285-50][^cite_note-Winds_of_Change_in_Nagorno_Karabakh-51][^cite_note-AMS-281] 4,210 Azerbaijani soldiers[^cite_note-OhanyanVelikhanova-282] and 749 civilians were also missing.[^cite_note-OhanyanVelikhanova-282] The total number of Azerbaijani civilians killed in the conflict is unknown, although 167–763 were killed on one day in 1992 by the Republic of Nagorno-Karabakh's forces.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-52]

### 1994–2019

Although no precise casualty figures exist, between 1994 and 2009, as many as 3,000 people, mostly soldiers, had been killed, according to most observers.[^cite_note-crisis3000-55] In 2008, the fighting became more intense and frequent.[^cite_note-285] With 72 deaths in 2014, the year became the bloodiest since the war had ended.[^cite_note-restraints-129] Two years later, between 1 and 5 April 2016, heavy fighting along the Nagorno-Karabakh front left 91 Armenian (11 non-combat)[^cite_note-web.archive.org-286][^cite_note-at92-139] and 94 Azerbaijani soldiers dead, with two missing.[^cite_note-meydan-140] In addition, 15 civilians (nine Armenians and six Azerbaijanis) were killed.[^cite_note-families-287][^cite_note-dictatorship-288]

Azerbaijan stated 398 of its soldiers and 31 civilians were killed between 1994 and up to September 2020, right before the start of the 2020 conflict.[^cite_note-289] In comparison, the Caspian Defense Studies Institute [NGO](46539.md) reported 1,008 Azerbaijani soldiers and more than 90 civilians were killed between 1994 and 2016.[^cite_note-290]

| 2009 | N/A | N/A | 19 soldiers[^cite_note-crisis-291] |
| 2010 | 7 soldiers[^cite_note-292] | 18 soldiers | 25 soldiers[^cite_note-crisis-291] |
| 2011 | 10 soldiers[^cite_note-tolldown-127] | 4+ soldiers,[^cite_note-crisis-291][^cite_note-deadly-126] 1 civilian[^cite_note-child-293] | 14+ soldiers, 1 civilian |

| 2017 | 22 soldiers[^cite_note-295] | 19 soldiers[^cite_note-296] | 41 soldiers |
