| 2019 | 4 soldiers[^cite_note-299] | 6+ soldiers[^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301] | 10+ soldiers |

### 2020–2023

Between January and September 2020, 16 Azerbaijani[^cite_note-302] and 8 Armenian soldiers,[^cite_note-303] as well as an Azerbaijani civilian, were killed in sporadic clashes.[^cite_note-304]

The [Second Nagorno-Karabakh War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Nagorno-Karabakh_War) occurred between 27 September and 10 November 2020. According to Azerbaijan, the fighting left 2,906 Azerbaijani soldiers[^cite_note-305] and 100 civilians dead,[^cite_note-306] while six servicemen were still missing.[^cite_note-307] Armenian authorities stated the fighting had left 3,825 Armenian soldiers[^cite_note-ArmenianKIA-167] and 85 civilians dead,[^cite_note-308] while 187 servicemen and 21 civilians were still missing.[^cite_note-ArmenianMIA-169] Additionally, the Syrian Observatory for Human Rights documented the deaths of 541 Syrian mercenaries fighting for Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-SOHR541-171] Two Russian soldiers were also killed when their helicopter was shot down by Azerbaijan by accident while flying in Armenian airspace near the border.[^cite_note-309] In addition, a 13-year-old Russian citizen was killed during an Armenian missile strike on the city of Ganja.[^cite_note-russianboy-310] In the two-month period fighting, thousands were killed, primarily soldiers, but also an estimated two hundred civilians.[^cite_note-311] Following the end of the war, eleven more Azerbaijani soldiers, six Azerbaijani civilians and one Russian peacekeeper were killed in clashes and landmine explosions in the region by the end of 2020.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315]

Twelve Azerbaijani civilians[^cite_note-316] and two soldiers were killed in 2021 by landmine explosions.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319] Seventeen Armenian[^cite_note-320] and ten Azerbaijani soldiers were also killed in shoot-outs in the border area,[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324] while 38 Armenian soldiers were captured.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326] Twenty-eight of the captured Armenian soldiers were subsequently released in December 2021.[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328]

In 2022, three Armenian soldiers were killed and 14 wounded in an attack by Azerbaijani drones in Nagorno-Karabakh on 25 March.[^cite_note-329]

During the [2023 Azerbaijani offensive in Nagorno-Karabakh](74857564.md), over 190 Azerbaijani and 190 Artsakh soldiers were killed.[^cite_note-\:02-330][^cite_note-331] 5 Russian peacekeepers were also killed.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-\:11-273] Subsequently, over 100,000 ethnic Armenian inhabitants of Nagorno-Karabakh [were expelled from the region](74922032.md).[^cite_note-\:32-333]

## Foreign involvement

### Countries

#### Russia



Azerbaijani President [Ilham Aliyev](22765442.md), Russian President [Dmitry Medvedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Medvedev) and Armenian President [Serzh Sargsyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serzh_Sargsyan) on 23 January 2012

Russia is officially neutral and has sought to play the role of a mediator.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336] In its official statements, Russia calls for a peaceful settlement and restraint during skirmishes.[^cite_note-337] British journalist Thomas de Waal has argued that there is an Azerbaijani narrative that Russia has "consistently supported the Armenian side." According to de Waal, Russia "has more supported the Armenian side," but there have been various "different Russian actors at different times supporting both sides in this conflict." He argues that President [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin) did not "want to see the Armenian side be defeated, but he also didn't want to supply them with too many weapons." De Waal concluded in 2012 that "Russia \[is] playing both sides", but "ultimately more in the Armenian side."[^cite_note-338] Other commentators have argued that Russia plays both sides in the conflict.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340] [Svante Cornell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svante_Cornell) argued in 2018 that Russia "had been playing both sides of the Armenia–Azerbaijan conflict to gain maximum control over both, a policy that continues to this day."[^cite_note-341]

During the first Nagorno-Karabakh War, Russia was widely viewed as supporting the Armenian position due to it providing Armenia with military assistance, including arms and indirect logistical support.[^cite_note-342][^cite_note-Panossian-33] Russia supplied around \$1 billion worth of weapons and, thus, "made a vital contribution to the Armenian victory."[^cite_note-Matveeva-32] According to de Waal, "greater Russian support for the Armenians" was one of the main factors behind the Armenian victory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Waal2003206-343] De Waal notes, "Yet it is not entirely clear how this support for the Armenians was translated on to the battlefield; to complicate things further, the Russians also gave some assistance to Azerbaijan."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Waal2003200-344]

Following the first Nagorno-Karabakh War and up until 2022, Russia was Armenia's main arms supplier and the two countries are military allies.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346] Russia is sometimes described as Armenia's supporter in the conflict,[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348] however, this view is widely challenged as Russia extensively sells arms to Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-Waal_MEI2020-60][^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351] Also, in February 2022, Aliyev and Putin signed a "Declaration of Allied Interaction" which elevated military ties between their countries and affirms "mutual respect for ... \[the] territorial integrity and inviolability of the borders of the two countries" and obliges Azerbaijan and Russia to "refrain from any actions, including those carried out through third states, directed against each other."[^cite_note-352][^cite_note-353] The agreement also has provisions for joint military operations and assistance between Russian and Azerbaijan, including personnel training and production, maintenance, repair, and modernization of military equipment.[^cite_note-354] However, up until 2022 Armenia purchased Russian weaponry at a discount, while Azerbaijan pays the full price.[^cite_note-355] In 2022, Russia suspended arms deliveries to Armenia.[^cite_note-356][^cite_note-\:12-357]

#### Turkey



Ilham Aliyev and Turkish President [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan) on 25 February 2020

Turkey is widely considered Azerbaijan's main supporter in the conflict.[^cite_note-TurkMain-358][^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360] Svante Cornell wrote in 1998 that Turkey is the "only country that constantly expressed its support for Azerbaijan."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECornell199851-361] It provided Azerbaijan "active military help" during the war.[^cite_note-362] Turkey also supports Azerbaijan diplomatically.[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364] Turkish and Azerbaijani armed forces cooperate extensively[^cite_note-365] and regularly hold [military exercises](1225714.md).[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] Azerbaijan has also bought weapons from Turkey.[^cite_note-TurkArmSup-368]

Turkey closed its border with Armenia in April 1993 after Armenian forces [captured Kalbajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kalbajar).[^cite_note-369] Prior to that, the border was only open "on demand and only for transferring the humanitarian aid (mainly wheat delivery) to Armenia and for the operation of the weekly Kars-Gyumri train, which had been crossing the Turkish–Armenian border since the days of the Soviet Union."[^cite_note-370] Turkey has repeatedly refused to normalize and establish diplomatic relations with Armenia in solidarity with Azerbaijan over Karabakh.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372] Turkey and Azerbaijan signed the "Shusha Declaration" in Shusha, a city that Azerbaijan captured during the Second Nagorno-Karabakh War.[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375] The declaration reaffirmed the military and economic cooperation between the countries and Turkish President Erdogan said the opening of a Turkish consulate in Shusha "would be a message to the world and especially to Armenia."[^cite_note-376]

#### Iran

[Iran](14653.md) is officially neutral and has sought to play the role of a mediator,[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] most notably [in 1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Communiqu%C3%A9). In its official statements, Iran calls for a peaceful settlement[^cite_note-379] and restraint during skirmishes.[^cite_note-380] At the same time, Iranian officials have repeatedly reaffirmed their support for Azerbaijan's territorial integrity.[^cite_note-386] Deputy Foreign Minister [Abbas Araghchi](21951274.md) stated in 2020 that "While respecting the territorial integrity of the Azerbaijan Republic, Iran is fundamentally opposed to any move that would fuel conflict between the two neighbouring countries of the Azerbaijan Republic and Armenia."[^cite_note-387]

During the war, "Iran was domestically torn in devising a policy", but *de facto* "pursued a policy that combined official neutrality with growing support for Armenia," according to Svante Cornell.[^cite_note-CornellIran-388] Cornell argues that Iran has "pursued policies in the conflict inclined towards Armenia."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECornell199851-361] However, Iran's tacit support for the Armenian side[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391] was limited to economic cooperation.[^cite_note-Dinu2010-392][^cite_note-393] [Terhi Hakala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terhi_Hakala) noted in 1998 that "as a geopolitical counter-weight to Turkey, Iran has strongly supported Armenia, especially by alleviating the effects of the Turkish blockade."[^cite_note-394] Cornell notes that during the war, Iran served as Armenia's "main purveyor of electricity and goods, and once the Armenian conquest of Karabakh had been completed, Iranian trucks began to supply most of the secessionist enclave's needs."[^cite_note-CornellIran-388] According to Bahruz Balayev, "Iran supported the territorial integrity of Azerbaijan and gave some humanitarian aid to the \[Azerbaijani] refugees, but in the meantime widely cooperates with Armenia and even Karabakh Armenian authorities."[^cite_note-395] [Brenda Shaffer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brenda_Shaffer) wrote that "Iran's cooperation with Armenia and its tacit support in the conflict with Azerbaijan over Karabagh strengthened Yerevan's actual and perceived power and consequently may have lessened its sense of urgency to resolve the conflict."[^cite_note-396]

In 2013, [Mohsen Rezaee](1862765.md), who was commander of the [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps](1285472.md) (IRGC) during the war, claimed that he "personally issued an order \[...] for the Republic of Azerbaijan army to be equipped appropriately and for it to receive the necessary training." Rezaee added that "Many Iranians died in the Karabakh War. In addition to the wounded, who were transported to \[Iran], many of the Iranian martyrs of the Karabakh War are buried in Baku."[^cite_note-397] In 2011, [Hassan Ameli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Ameli), a leading Iranian cleric, claimed that Iran provided Azerbaijan with arms and helped the [Afghan mujaheddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen) move to Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399] The Iranian embassy in Armenia stated that they would not like unreliable information to affect friendly Armenian-Iranian relations: "We do not exclude the possibility that there are forces, which aim to create hindrances for our friendly relations."[^cite_note-400] In October 2020, several protests erupted in Iranian cities, including the capital [Tehran](57654.md) and [Tabriz](439791.md), in support of Azerbaijan,[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402] with many [Iranian Azerbaijanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Azerbaijanis) chanting pro-Azerbaijan slogans and protesting Iran's alleged arms support to Armenia via the [Nordooz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nurduz) border crossing.[^cite_note-403]

#### United States



[Joseph Dunford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Dunford), chairman of the [Joint Chiefs of Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Chiefs_of_Staff), with Azerbaijani Minister of Defense [Zakir Hasanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakir_Hasanov) on 16 February 2017

[Thomas Ambrosio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Ambrosio) suggested in 2000 that the US "supported Azerbaijan's territorial integrity, but enacted policies that effectively supported Armenia's irredentist policies."[^cite_note-404] [Sergo Mikoyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergo_Mikoyan) argued in 1998 that the US response to the conflict has been "inconsistent, pulled in different directions by the legislative and executive branches of power." Congress was under the influence of the Armenian lobby, while the executive branch (the White House and the State Department) pursued a pro-Azerbaijani policy, which "reflects Turkish influence and the interests of oil companies."[^cite_note-405] [Richard C. Longworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_C._Longworth) and Argam DerHartunian expressed similar views.[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407]

[Congress](31756.md)'s pro-Armenian position was expressed in passing the [Section 907](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_907) of the [Freedom Support Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Support_Act) in 1992, which banned any assistance to Azerbaijan. It was effectively amended by the Senate in 2001 and waived by President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) starting from 2002.[^cite_note-408] The US provides [military aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_military_aid) to both countries. Between 2005 and 2016 Azerbaijan received \$8.5 million for counternarcotics assistance and \$11.5 million for counterterrorism aid. In the same period, Armenia received only \$41,000 for counternarcotics assistance and none for counterterrorism aid. According to [EurasiaNet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EurasiaNet), "Much of the money for Azerbaijan has been targeted toward naval forces, to reduce the risk that it could be used against Armenia."[^cite_note-409] The [Trump administration](52231341.md) greatly increased the US military aid to Azerbaijan to around \$100 million in fiscal years 2018–19, compared to less than \$3 million in a year in FY 2016–17. The US aid is primarily "offered in the context of U.S. policy to increase pressure on Iran and focuses on Azerbaijan's Iranian border, but it also has implications for Armenia," according to Emil Sanamyan. In FY 2018, Armenia received \$4.2 million in U.S. security assistance.[^cite_note-410]

The US has also provided humanitarian aid to Artsakh (some \$36 million between 1998 and 2010),[^cite_note-411] including for [demining](2206580.md).[^cite_note-412] The humanitarian aid has been criticized by Azerbaijan for legitimizing the "illegal regime in the occupied lands and damages the reputation of the US as a neutral mediator."[^cite_note-413]

### Arms suppliers

In 1992, the OSCE "requested its participating states to impose an embargo on arms deliveries to forces engaged in combat in the Nagorno-Karabakh area." However, it is a "voluntary multilateral arms embargo, and a number of OSCE participating states have supplied arms to Armenia and Azerbaijan since 1992."[^cite_note-414] The [UN Security Council Resolution 853](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_853), passed in July 1993, called on states to "refrain from the supply of any weapons and munitions which might lead to an intensification of the conflict or the continued occupation of territory." According to [SIPRI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stockholm_International_Peace_Research_Institute), "since 2002, the UN Security Council has no longer listed that it is 'actively seized of the matter'. As such, since 2002, it is assumed that the non-mandatory UN embargo is no longer active."[^cite_note-415]

#### Armenia

Russia has long been Armenia's primary arms supplier. Smaller suppliers include [China](5405.md),[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417] [India](14533.md),[^cite_note-418][^cite_note-419] [Ukraine](31750.md),[^cite_note-Ukr2011-420] [Greece](12108.md),[^cite_note-421][^cite_note-Dinu2010-392] [Serbia](29265.md),[^cite_note-422] Jordan (per Armenian MoD sources,[^cite_note-423] denied by Jordan).[^cite_note-424] In March 1992, [Yagub Mammadov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yagub_Mammadov_\(politician\)), chairman of Azerbaijani parliament, accused Syria and Lebanon of supplying weapons to Armenia.[^cite_note-425]

#### Azerbaijan

According to SIPRI, Russia supplied 55% of Azerbaijan's weaponry in 2007–11,[^cite_note-426] 85% in 2010–14[^cite_note-427] and 31% in 2015–19.[^cite_note-Sipri2020-428] [Israel](9282173.md) has become a major supplier,[^cite_note-429] accounting for 60% of Azerbaijan's arms imports in 2015–19.[^cite_note-Sipri2020-428] Azerbaijan's other suppliers include [Turkey](11125639.md),[^cite_note-TurkArmSup-368] [Belarus](3457.md),[^cite_note-430] [Canada](5042916.md) (via Turkey),[^cite_note-Can2020-431][^cite_note-Can30Oct2020-432] Ukraine,[^cite_note-Ukr2011-420][^cite_note-433] Serbia,[^cite_note-434] and [Czech Republic](5321.md)[^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436] (denied by the Czech authorities).[^cite_note-437]

### Foreign fighters

Several foreign groups fought on both sides in the intense period of fighting in 1992–94. According to [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) (HRW), both sides used [mercenaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercenaries) during the war, namely "Russian, Ukrainian, and Belorussian mercenaries or rogue units of the Soviet/Russian Army have fought on both sides."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW1994106-438]

#### Azerbaijan

Azerbaijan made extensive use of mercenary pilots. According to a 1994 report from Human Rights Watch, "Most informed observers believe that mercenaries pilot most of Azerbaijan's air force."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW1994110-439]

Several foreign groups fought on the Azerbaijani side: Chechen militants, Afghan mujahideen,[^cite_note-440] members of the [Turkish ultranationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_nationalism) [Grey Wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey_Wolves_\(organization\)),[^cite_note-441] and the [Ukrainian nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_nationalism) and neo-fascist [UNA-UNSO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_National_Assembly_%E2%80%93_Ukrainian_People's_Self-Defence).[^cite_note-442] The Chechen fighters in Karabakh were led by [Shamil Basayev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamil_Basayev), who later became [Prime Minister of Ichkeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Ichkeria) (Chechnya), and [Salman Raduyev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salman_Raduyev).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaarnby20089-443] Basayev famously participated in the [battle of Shusha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Shusha_\(1992\)) in 1992.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaarnby20089-443][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Waal2003179-444] Saudi-born [Ibn al-Khattab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Khattab) may have also joined them.[^cite_note-445] The Afghan mujahideen were mostly affiliated with the [Hezb-e Islami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezb-e_Islami_Gulbuddin), led by Afghan Prime Minister [Gulbuddin Hekmatyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulbuddin_Hekmatyar).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaarnby20086-446][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW199481-447] According to HRW, they were "clearly not motivated by religious or ideological reasons" and were, thus, mercenaries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW199481-447] The recruitment of Afghan mujahideen, reportedly handled by [paramilitary police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Purpose_Police_Unit) chief [Rovshan Javadov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rovshan_Javadov), was denied by Azerbaijani authorities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW199481-447][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaarnby20085–6-448] They first arrived to Azerbaijan in fall 1993 and numbered anywhere between 1,500 and 2,500[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW199481-447] or 1,000 and 3,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaarnby20087-449] Armenia alleged that they were paid for by Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaarnby20086-446] Afghan mujahideen constituted the most considerable influx of foreign fighters during the war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaarnby20087-449] Some 200 Grey Wolves were still present in the conflict zone as of September 1994 and were engaged in training Azerbaijani units.[^cite_note-450]

#### Artsakh and Armenia

Some 85 Russian [Kuban Cossacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuban_Cossack) and around 30 [Ossetian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ossetians) volunteers fought on the Armenian side.[^cite_note-451][^cite_note-452] In May 2011, a *[khachkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khachkar)* was inaugurated in the village of [Vank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vank,_Karabakh) in memory of 14 Kuban Cossacks who died in the war.[^cite_note-453] Ossetian volunteers reportedly came from both [South Ossetia](318381.md) (Georgia) and [North Ossetia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Ossetia) (Russia).[^cite_note-454][^cite_note-455] No less than 12 [diaspora Armenian](214413.md) volunteers fought and four diaspora fighters died in the war.[^cite_note-456][^cite_note-457] According to [David Rieff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Rieff), members of the [Armenian Revolutionary Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Revolutionary_Federation) (Dashnaks), "including a substantial number of volunteers from the diaspora, did a great deal of the fighting and dying."[^cite_note-458] Former members of the [Armenian Secret Army for the Liberation of Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Secret_Army_for_the_Liberation_of_Armenia) (ASALA) also participated in the war.[^cite_note-459]

### Diplomatic support

#### Artsakh and Armenia

Artsakh (Nagorno-Karabakh Republic) received diplomatic recognition[^cite_note-460] and diplomatic support, especially during the 2016 clashes, from three partially recognized states: [Abkhazia](18933375.md),[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462][^cite_note-463] [South Ossetia](318381.md),[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465] and [Transnistria](376581.md).[^cite_note-466][^cite_note-467]

During the war, Greece adopted a pro-Armenian position[^cite_note-468] and supported it in international forums.[^cite_note-469][^cite_note-Dinu2010-392] During the April 2016 and July 2020 clashes, [Cyprus](5593.md) condemned Azerbaijan for violating the ceasefire.[^cite_note-470][^cite_note-471]

Armenian President [Levon Ter-Petrosyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levon_Ter-Petrosyan) reportedly told the Greek ambassador in 1993 that France and Russia were Armenia's only allies at the time.[^cite_note-472] According to a US State Department cable released in 2020, the French ambassador to the UN, [Jean-Bernard Mérimée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Bernard_M%C3%A9rim%C3%A9e), succeeded in changing the wording of the [UNSC Resolution 822](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_822) to state that it was "local Armenian forces", not "Armenian forces" that occupied Kalbajar. He also suggested treating the Armenian capture of Kalbajar not under [Chapter VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapter_VII_of_the_United_Nations_Charter) of the [UN Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Charter) (an act of aggression), but [Chapter VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapter_VI_of_the_United_Nations_Charter) (a dispute that should be settled peacefully).[^cite_note-473]

#### Azerbaijan

Azerbaijan has received explicit diplomatic support in the conflict from several countries and international organizations. Azerbaijan's strongest diplomatic supporters are Turkey and Pakistan,[^cite_note-474][^cite_note-475] which is the only UN member state not to have recognized Armenia's independence in support for Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-476] Turkish-backed unrecognized [Northern Cyprus](271409.md) (Turkish Cyprus) also supports Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-477] The [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md) (OIC)[^cite_note-478] and the [Turkic Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_Council)[^cite_note-479] have repeatedly supported the Azerbaijani position. Some member states of these organizations, namely [Uzbekistan](31853.md)[^cite_note-480] and [Saudi Arabia](349303.md)[^cite_note-481] have voiced support for Azerbaijan's position on their own repeatedly. Lebanon, on the other hand, has not supported OIC's pro-Azerbaijani resolutions.[^cite_note-482]

Azerbaijan has received diplomatic support, namely for its territorial integrity, from three post-Soviet states that have territorial disputes: Ukraine,[^cite_note-483] Georgia,[^cite_note-484] and Moldova.[^cite_note-485] These three countries and Azerbaijan form the [GUAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GUAM_Organization_for_Democracy_and_Economic_Development) organization and support the Azerbaijani position in the format as well.[^cite_note-GUAM-486] Serbia, with its own territorial dispute over [Kosovo](17391.md), also explicitly supports Azerbaijan's territorial integrity.[^cite_note-487][^cite_note-488][^cite_note-489] Two other post-Soviet states, [Kazakhstan](16642.md)[^cite_note-490] and Belarus[^cite_note-491] tacitly support Azerbaijan's position, especially within the [Eurasian Economic Union](27290106.md) (EEU) and the CSTO, despite nominal alliance with Armenia.[^cite_note-492] Both [Palestine](241405.md)[^cite_note-493] and Israel[^cite_note-494][^cite_note-495] have voiced support for Azerbaijan. China has supported Azerbaijan's position on Nagorno-Karabakh, which has been reciprocated by Azerbaijan's support of China on Taiwan.[^cite_note-496]

#### 2008 UN vote

On 14 March 2008, the United Nations General Assembly adopted a resolution which "reaffirmed Azerbaijan's territorial integrity, expressing support for that country's internationally recognized borders and demanding the immediate withdrawal of all Armenian forces from all occupied territories there." It was adopted by a vote of 39 in favor to 7 against, while most countries either abstained or were absent. It was backed mostly by Muslim states[^cite_note-497][^cite_note-498] (31 were [members of the OIC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_states_of_the_Organisation_of_Islamic_Cooperation)).[^cite_note-499] Non-Muslim states that supported the resolution included three post-Soviet states: Georgia, Moldova, Ukraine, and five other nations: Cambodia, Colombia, Myanmar, Serbia, and Tuvalu. Thus, it was supported by seven OSCE members;[^cite_note-500] one [NATO](21133.md) member (Turkey) and no EU member state.[^cite_note-2008UNvote-501]

It was opposed by [Angola](701.md), Armenia, [France](5843419.md), [India](14533.md), Russia, the United States, and [Vanuatu](32443.md).[^cite_note-2008UNvote-501] The OSCE Minsk Group co-chair countries (France, US, Russia) voted against the resolution. They argued that it "selectively propagates only certain of \[the basic] principles to the exclusion of others, without considering the Co-Chairs' proposal in its balanced entirety." The co-chair countries called it a unilateral resolution, which "threatens to undermine the peace process," but reaffirmed their "support for the territorial integrity of Azerbaijan, and thus do not recognize the independence of NK."[^cite_note-502]

## Azerbaijani laundromat

In 2017, an [Azerbaijani laundromat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijani_laundromat) [money-laundering](19390.md) scheme organized by Azerbaijan was revealed by the [OCCRP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organized_Crime_and_Corruption_Reporting_Project). The report revealed that between 2012 and 2014, Azerbaijan created a [slush fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slush_fund) of US\$2.9 billion used to bribe European and American politicians, journalists, lawmakers, and academics to lobby for Azerbaijani interests abroad, including promoting a pro-Azerbaijan agenda for the Nagorno-Karabakh conflict. This form of bribery has been referred to as "[caviar diplomacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caviar_diplomacy)".[^cite_note-baku-america-503][^cite_note-Canbäck-504][^cite_note-Mondani-Ciccolella-505] The laundering scheme has operated by wiring millions of euros into the private bank accounts of influential Western figures and by providing them with luxurious trips to Azerbaijan. The [European Azerbaijani Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Azerbaijani_Society?action=edit\&redlink=1) (TEAS) lobbying group has played a large role in this by hiring European PR professionals, pariliment members, and former ministers.[^cite_note-506]

Azerbaijani-American businessman Adil Baguirov had been lobbying in Washington through secret funding from Azerbaijan’s state oil company since 2013. Baguirov runs the non-profit Houston-based US Azeris Network, which received a \$253,150 transfer after organizing and hosting a conference in Baku attended by 10 American members of Congress. In 2003, Baguirov began working as Special Advisor on Russia and the former Soviet Union to Congressman [Curt Weldon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curt_Weldon). Weldon and another Congressman, [Solomon Ortiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_Ortiz), both founded the Congressional Azerbaijan Caucus in 2004. The Congressional Azerbaijan Caucus was a frequent recipient of Azerbaijani laundromat funds. From 2008 to 2016, Baguirov was invited almost annually by the [United States House Appropriations Subcommittee on State, Foreign Operations, and Related Programs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Appropriations_Subcommittee_on_State,_Foreign_Operations,_and_Related_Programs) to suggest economic and military aid budgets for Azerbaijan and Armenia. In 2008, Baguirov lobbied for greater aid to be given to Azerbaijan, citing equity and neutrality. But by 2012, Baguirov lobbied for aid to Armenia to be reduced to zero, while requesting that Azerbaijan be granted \$26 million from [USAID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Agency_for_International_Development) and \$3.9 million in military aid.[^cite_note-baku-america-503]

The 2012 laundromat investigation revealed several bank transfers in 2012, totalling more than US\$9 million, made to the Hungarian [MKB Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MKB_Bank) account in [Budapest](36787.md) right around the time when, amid international controversy, the Hungarian government extradited the convicted Azerbaijani murderer [Ramil Safarov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramil_Safarov) to Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-507] Several media outlets suggested a connection between [Viktor Orbán](750582.md)'s visit to Baku in June and the first instalment of \$7.6 million transferred to the bank account in July, since by the end of August Safarov was handed over to Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-508][^cite_note-509][^cite_note-510][^cite_note-511][^cite_note-512][^cite_note-513]

In 2017, following a series of critical reports and concern expressed by many members of the [Parliamentary Assembly of the Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_Assembly_of_the_Council_of_Europe) (PACE), the Assembly's Bureau decided to set up an independent, external body to investigate allegations of corruption in PACE.[^cite_note-514][^cite_note-515] The investigation body's final report was published in April 2018,[^cite_note-516] finding "strong suspicions of corruptive conduct involving members of the Assembly" and naming a number of members and former members as having breached the Assembly's Code of Conduct. Many of the members or former members mentioned in the report were sanctioned: four members[^cite_note-517] were deprived of certain rights and 14 members,[^cite_note-518] accused of accepting gifts and bribes from the government of Azerbaijan, were expelled from the Assembly's premises for life.[^cite_note-519] One named individual was German politician and former [Parliamentary State Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_State_Secretary) [Eduard Lintner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduard_Lintner). It was revealed Lintner had lobbied on behalf of the Azerbaijani government since as early as 2009 when he founded the Society for Promoting German-Azerbaijani Relations.[^cite_note-520]

## Major ceasefire agreements and international mediation

Further information: [Madrid Principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Principles), [Tehran Communiqué](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Communiqu%C3%A9), and [Zheleznovodsk Communiqué](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zheleznovodsk_Communiqu%C3%A9)

Two major armistices occurred upon following the First and Second Nagorno-Karabakh War. Both ceasefires were brokered by Russia, with the first also being mediated by the [OSCE Minsk Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OSCE_Minsk_Group) (Russia, US, France).

### Bishkek Protocol

Main article: [Bishkek Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishkek_Protocol)

A Russian-brokered ceasefire was signed in May 1994 and peace talks, mediated by the OSCE Minsk Group have been held ever since by Armenia and Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-Freizer-2015-119] The 1994 Bishkek Protocol called for both sides to cease hostilities and engage in dialogue aimed at demilitarization of the region, return of refugees, and the creation of a [CIS](36870.md) peacekeeping force.[^cite_note-521][^cite_note-522][^cite_note-523] Azerbaijan has repeatedly accused the Minsk Group (Russia, US, France) of being pro-Armenian.[^cite_note-524][^cite_note-525][^cite_note-526] In 1996, when France was chosen by the OSCE to co-chair the Minsk Group, Azerbaijan asked the OSCE to reconsider the decision because France was perceived by Azerbaijan as pro-Armenian.[^cite_note-527] Svante Cornell argued in 1997 that France, the US and Russia are "more or less biased towards Armenia in the conflict."[^cite_note-528][^cite_note-529] In 2018 Azerbaijan accused the US and France of bias for allowing [Bako Sahakyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bako_Sahakyan), the then president of Artsakh, to visit their countries.[^cite_note-530][^cite_note-531]

### 2020 ceasefire agreement

Main article: [2020 Nagorno-Karabakh ceasefire agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Nagorno-Karabakh_ceasefire_agreement)

On 9 November 2020, Armenia and Azerbaijan signed a ceasefire under mediation from Russia, with the Republic of Artsakh also agreeing to end hostilities.[^cite_note-ria.ru-532] According to the agreement, both sides retained control of the positions they held at midnight. Armenia returned the territories surrounding Nagorno-Karabakh that it had occupied since the 1990s. Azerbaijan also retained control over one-third of Nagorno-Karabakh proper that it had captured during the war: including [Shusha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shusha_District_\(NKAO\)) and [Hadrut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrut_District_\(NKAO\)).[^cite_note-Geybullayeva-2023-72][^cite_note-533][^cite_note-534][^cite_note-535] In total, the Armenian side lost roughly 75% of the territories in and around Nagorno-Karabakh that it controlled prior to the war.[^cite_note-536] Until 2020, an independent Azerbaijan had never exercised de facto control over the region.[^cite_note-537][^cite_note-538][^cite_note-539][^cite_note-540][^cite_note-541][^cite_note-542]

Approximately 2,000 Russian soldiers were deployed as [peacekeeping forces around Nagorno-Karabakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peacekeeping_operations_in_Nagorno-Karabakh) with a mandate of at least five years. The peacekeepers were also given mandate over the Lachin corridor which following remains the only passage between Armenia and Nagorno-Karabakh. The alternative [Vardenis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vardenis)–Martakert route has been closed by Azerbaijan since it took control of the [Dadivank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dadivank)-[Sotk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sotk) section (in [Kalbajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalbajar)) during the Second Nagorno-Karabakh War (2020).[^cite_note-543][^cite_note-UNHCR-2020-544] As per the agreement, Armenia returned all territory it had occupied around Nagorno-Karabakh and Azerbaijan retained control over one-third of Nagorno-Karabakh proper that it captured. The 2020 agreement gave Russian peacekeepers a temporary but renewable mandate to the region.

Following the 2020 Nagorno-Karabakh War, both Russia and the European Union have increased their presence in Armenia along the border with Azerbaijan in order to improve stability of the border and deter offensives from Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-caucasuswatch.de-200][^cite_note-545] Upon Armenia's request, Russia's Federal Security Service expanded its patrols within Armenia and the EU contributed a civilian monitoring mission. However, while both entities have deterred the possibility of full-scale warfare, they have been unable to fully prevent Azerbaijan's goals.[^cite_note-caucasuswatch.de-200]

Russia and the European Union have criticized each other's presence within Armenia. The European Union encouraged Armenia to seek alternative security alliances given "Russia's alleged readiness to guarantee the security of Armenia has proven to be non-existent."[^cite_note-Kovatchev-0036-546] Russia, in turn, criticized the EU and Armenia for implementing the civilian monitoring in Armenia and claims it is an effort by the West to diminish Russia's power in the region.[^cite_note-547][^cite_note-548]

### 2023 ceasefire agreement

Main article: [2023 Nagorno-Karabakh ceasefire agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Nagorno-Karabakh_ceasefire_agreement)

A day after the resuming of hostilities, a ceasefire agreement was announced with the government of the Republic of Artsakh agreed to disarm and to enter into talks with the government of Azerbaijan regarding the reintegration of the territory.[^cite_note-549]

### 2024 Russian peacekeepers withdrawal

On 12 June 2024, the [Russian peacekeepers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peacekeeping_operations_in_Nagorno-Karabakh) completed their withdrawal from the Nagorno-Karabakh region which had started in April that year, following an agreement between President Putin and his Azerbaijani counterpart President Aliyev.[^cite_note-550]

### 2025 Armenia–Azerbaijan peace agreement



Azerbaijani President [Ilham Aliyev](22765442.md), US President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) and Armenian Prime Minister [Nikol Pashinyan](17897961.md) signing a trilateral joint declaration in Washington, D.C., 8 August 2025

On 8 August 2025, Armenian Prime Minister [Nikol Pashinyan](17897961.md) and Azerbaijani President [Ilham Aliyev](22765442.md) signed [a peace agreement](79518584.md) in a ceremony hosted by US President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) in the [White House](33057.md),[^cite_note-551] laying groundwork to end the more than 35-year conflict between Armenia and Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-552]

A comprehensive settlement remains elusive due to certain elements:[^cite_note-\:9-84] Azerbaijan’s refusal to guarantee the [right of return](74922032.md#Azerbaijan's_denial_of_the_Right_of_Return) for displaced Armenians, alongside ongoing disputes over detainees, including former Nagorno-Karabakh officials whose prosecution has drawn international criticism.[^cite_note-\:5-80] Negotiations are further complicated by Azerbaijan’s demands that Armenia concede an [extraterritorial transit corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zangezur_corridor)[^cite_note-\:6-81] and amend its constitution and national symbols.[^cite_note-\:7-82][^cite_note-\:8-83] Meanwhile, Armenia has not withdrawn international lawsuits against Azerbaijan.

## Political status

Main article: [Political status of Nagorno-Karabakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_status_of_Nagorno-Karabakh)

The political status of Nagorno-Karabakh remained unresolved from its declaration of independence from the Soviet Union in 1991 until its dissolution in 2024.[^cite_note-553][^cite_note-554] Within the Soviet Union, the region was an ethnic Armenian [autonomous oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_oblast) of the Azerbaijan Soviet Socialist Republic. From 1994 to 2024, the United Nations Security Council, OSCE Minsk Group, and other bodies made various statements and proposed dialogue initiatives; none of them successful. The Republic of Artsakh has not been recognized by any country, including Armenia, although international mediators and human rights organizations have emphasized self-determination for the local Armenian population, both as an internationally recognized right but also as a form of genocide prevention.[^cite_note-555][^cite_note-wilsoncenter.org-556][^cite_note-ReferenceE-557]

Following the 2020 Nagorno-Karabakh War, Azerbaijan has rescinded its offer of special status or autonomy to its ethnic Armenian residents and instead insisted on their "integration" into Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-Isayev-2022-558][^cite_note-Vock-2023b-559] In 2023, Azerbaijani President Aliyev said that Armenian residents of Nagorno-Karabakh must be "reintegrated" as "normal citizen\[s] of Azerbaijan" and that "the \[special] status \[for Armenians] went to hell. It failed; it was shattered to smithereens. It is not and will not be there. As long as I am president, there will be no status."[^cite_note-560] Aliyev also threatened military action.[^cite_note-ReferenceB-561][^cite_note-Aghayev-2023-562]

Despite being offered Azerbaijani citizenship, Artsakh residents did not trust Azerbaijan's guarantees of security due to the [country's history of human rights abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Azerbaijan), [Armenophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Armenian_sentiment_in_Azerbaijan) and lack of rights to ethnic minorities.[^cite_note-Kitachayev-2023-226]\[^cite_note-[www.wilsoncenter.org-2023-563](http://www.wilsoncenter.org-2023-563/)][^cite_note-Vock-2023a-564] Various human rights observers, scholars specializing in genocide studies, and politicians considered the blockade of Artsakh a form of ethnic cleansing and warned of the risk of genocide.[^cite_note-Genocide_Watch-2022-264][^cite_note-Genocide_Warning:_Nagorno_Karabakh-265][^cite_note-Lemkin-2023b-266][^cite_note-Chidiac-2023a-267][^cite_note-Rhodes-2023a-268][^cite_note-Gregorian-2022a-269][^cite_note-Politico-2023a-270] Many international observers also did not consider Azerbaijan's claim that Artsakh Armenians could live safely under Aliyev's regime to be credible.[^cite_note-intellinews-2023-565][^cite_note-Boy-2023-566][^cite_note-Civilnet-2023-567][^cite_note-Hauer-2023-568][^cite_note-ReferenceE-557]

Political analyst Eric Hacopian has said that "Expecting Armenians to submit to the rule of a nationalistic and undemocratic Azeri government] is the equivalent of asking 100,000 Israelis to live under Hamas."[^cite_note-\:12-357] Caucasus expert Laurence Broers wrote "the blockade \[of Nagorno-Karabakh] renders irrelevant any talk of the civil integration of Karabakh Armenians. It vindicates the worst fears of the Karabakh Armenian population."[^cite_note-Civilnet-2023-567] Political analysts predict that Azerbaijan would arbitrarily detain and torture civilians, under the pretext of their association with the Artsakh government or with previous wars, if it took control over the region.[^cite_note-Hauer-2023-568] At least two incidents of Azerbaijani forces detaining Armenian residents around Azerbaijan's military checkpoint have been confirmed.[^cite_note-Shahverdyan-2023a-569][^cite_note-570]

Georgia continues to claim the area as its own territory, designating it as [Russian-occupied territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupied_territories_of_Georgia).

[^cite_note-464]: &#x20;[South Ossetia](318381.md)'s status is disputed. The [Georgian](48768.md) government and most of the world's other states consider South Ossetia *[de jure](43594.md)* a part of Georgia's territory.

[^cite_note-466]: &#x20;[Transnistria](376581.md)'s [political status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_status_of_Transnistria) is disputed. It considers itself to be an [independent state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_state), but [this is not recognised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_recognition_of_Transnistria) by any [UN member state](31969.md). The [Moldovan](19260.md) government and the international community consider Transnistria a part of Moldova's territory.

In 1923, within the territorial borders of Azerbaijan, Mountainous Karabakh was given autonomy, and the regions of Shamkhar, Khanlar, Dashkesan and Shahumian, where the Armenian population is predominant (approximately 90 per cent), were directly incorporated into the Azerbaijan SSR. Thus, the Armenians of Karabagh were cut off from the Armenian SSR and Mountainous Karabakh Autonomous Republic failed to include all Armenian regions. The rights of the autonomous region were gradually curtailed and presently are almost entirely abrogated. The Armenian population of the Azerbaijani SSR has been subjected to chauvinistic policies creating extremely unfavorable conditions of life.

[^cite_note-:0-92]: &#x20;[Saparov 2014](#CITEREFSaparov2014), p. 116: "The solution proposed by the \[Karabakh uezdy] conference was similar to previous suggestions, but it also called for the creation of a Karabakh Oblast’, a region that would encompass both mountainous and lowland parts of Karabakh. It offered a way in which the creation of a predominantly Armenian autonomous unit in the mountainous part of the region could be avoided. During 1921, the position of the Azerbaijani leadership regarding Karabakh autonomy had been that suppression of banditry in the region would solve the ethnic conflict, and that there would be no need to grant autonomy to the Armenian-populated part of Karabakh. This tactic was successful in the short term."

[^cite_note-Levon_Chorbajian,_Patrick_Donabédian,_Claude_Mutafian-93]: &#x20;[Chorbajian, Mutafian & Donabedian 1994](#CITEREFChorbajianMutafianDonabedian1994), pp. 138–140, 154: "The borders were to be drawn before 15 August by a mixed commission...but without the participation of either Yerevan or Moscow. All would be presided over by Karaiev. Under such circumstances, the Armenians could expect to be grossly disappointed. On the one hand...they excluded, on the west, the 'corridor' made up of Lachin, Kelbajar, and Kedabek, which had been carefully emptied of its Armenian population to separate Mountainous Karabagh from Armenian Zangezur. On the other hand, in the north, without any justification, they removed the districts of Shamkhor, Khanlar, Dashkesan and Shahumian.. where the Armenian population was predominant (about 90 per cent)... From Shamkhor in the north to Shahumian in the south, Armenian villages in these districts have been systematically emptied...Mountainous Karabagh delimited in this way is only a portion of what had always been Armenian Karabagh, which itself is only a part of what was included in the ancient Armenian provinces of Artsakh and Utik...The spectre of ‘Nakhichevanization’ haunts...Mountainous Karabagh, which had 125,000 inhabitants in 1926 who were 89 percent Armenian. This region has become an ‘enclave’ since the ‘cleansing’ of the Hagaru Valley in order to separate Karabagh from Zangezur by a narrow strip emptied of Armenians...Azerbaijan still contained a large Armenian minority. Aside from the ‘bastion’ of the Autonomous Region of Mountainous Karabagh, Armenians were numerous in Baku and in the region north of the Autonomous Region, up to Shamkhor, where the Armenian villages had been deliberately left outside the frontiers drawn in 1923 and, thereby, subjected to direct Azerbaijani authority. From north to south, these areas had already largely been ‘swept clean,’ with the exception of the area of Shahumian, the northern gateway to the Autonomous Region. The Azerbaijani plan was clearly described in the declaration by the Karabagh Committee on 2 December 1988: 'Exploiting the anarchic situation, the Azerbaijani authorities are about to unleash a monstrous programme: to expel Armenians from their several millennia old homes in Gandzak and the areas north of Artsakh, in preparation for an invasion of Mountainous Karabagh.' Already about 120,000 Armenians have left Azerbaijan, and 50,000 have sought refuge in Armenia and the others in the North Caucasus and Central Asia."

Nagorno-Karabakh petitioned for the opening of an institute of higher education there. \[In Azerbaijan] everybody was against it. In Stepanakert itself, there was no workforce, so we sent Azerbaijanis from the surrounding districts. With these and other measures, I tried to increase the number of Azerbaijanis in Nagorno-Karabakh, and the number of Armenians decreased. The Republic of Nagorno-Karabakh is one of four frozen conflicts that emerged after the collapse of the Soviet Union. Legally recognized as a part of Azerbaijan, it declared independence in 1991 and then defended that independence in a war with Azerbaijan that lasted until 1994.
